    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F51.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess technically occupational health sent me here. I work in metal and plastics fabrication, running presses and doing shop-floor stuff all day, and they said my weight is dangerously low for the kind of work I’m doing. They seemed pretty concerned about safety, like me being on the floor around machines and lifting and moving things.\n\nI don’t know, I honestly didn’t think of it that way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had things under control. So it feels a little strange being here because I didn’t exactly decide on my own that something was wrong, but they made it sound like I needed to be checked out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally limiting the amount of food you eat or trying to lose weight, and if so, why?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, I have been limiting it. I don’t usually think of it as “trying to lose weight” exactly, or at least I don’t say that to myself. It feels more like… keeping things contained, I guess.\n\nMost days before my early shift I just have coffee, because if I eat in the morning it feels like I’ve already messed up the whole day before it even starts. Then at lunch I might have half a protein bar, not always the whole thing, just enough to get through. And after work, if I go to yoga, I’ll usually have a small bowl of vegetables afterward. Something plain, something I can measure in my head and not feel like it’s going to spiral.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding ridiculous, but eating more than that makes the whole day feel out of control. Like once I start, I don’t trust where it stops, and then I’m thinking about it for hours. So keeping it small and predictable makes me feel calmer, like I’m disciplined and on top of things. That’s the reason, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: When you talk about feeling like things would spiral if you eat more, are you mainly worried about gaining weight or changes to your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… if I’m being honest, it is about gaining weight. I don’t always want to admit that because it sounds shallow, and I tell myself it’s more about discipline or routine or feeling in control, but the second the scale goes up even a little, I panic.\n\nLike, even a small change — something anyone else would probably say is nothing — my brain immediately goes, “This is it, you’re losing control, it’s all going to come back, you’re going to get heavy again.” And then I can’t stop thinking about it. It feels like all the work I’ve done to keep myself contained could just disappear if I let up at all.\n\nSo yes, body shape too, I guess, but the scale is the thing that makes it feel real. If the number goes up, I feel like I’ve failed and I have to tighten everything back down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives after eating, or push yourself to exercise more than you should to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that neatly.\n\nI don’t really think of it in those exact categories, like “making myself sick” or “using laxatives” or whatever. That’s not something I’ve been keeping track of in the way I track food or the scale. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but it feels hard because some things I do feel normal to me, and then when someone says it back in a medical-sounding way it suddenly sounds more serious.\n\nWith exercise… I do get anxious if I don’t move enough, and I can get pretty rigid about it. Like if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel this pressure in my head that I need to compensate somehow. But I don’t know where the line is between “I like being active” and “more than I should.” I work a physical job already, so it’s all kind of mixed together in my mind.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I do pay a lot of attention to making sure I don’t feel like food is just sitting there with no way to balance it out. But I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to prevent weight gain or to feel like you’ve ‘cleansed’ after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that in a way that’s useful.\n\nI’m not saying yes, exactly. I don’t have some clear thing where I can say, “Oh, I do this many times a week,” or “I use this to undo eating.” It’s not like that in my head. And I don’t want to say something wrong and have it sound like I’m admitting to something if I’m not even sure what counts.\n\nI understand what you mean by making yourself vomit, but… I guess I don’t think of myself as someone who does that. And with laxatives, I’m not sure what you mean by “used” — like taking them because you’re constipated, or taking them because you feel awful after eating, or taking more than you’re supposed to? Because those feel like different things to me, and I don’t really have a neat answer.\n\nThe “cleansed” part is hard too, because I do understand that feeling of wanting food to be gone, or wanting to feel empty again, but I don’t know that I can connect it to those specific things you’re asking about. I think I mostly just get stuck in my head afterward, like trying to figure out how to make the feeling pass. So… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if that’s not accurate.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or taken laxatives specifically to try to control your weight or shape, even if it was just once or twice?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to make it more specific, but I still don’t feel like I can give you a clean yes-or-no.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory where I think, “Yes, I did that specifically to change my weight or shape.” At least not in a way I can confidently say out loud. I know I’ve had times after eating where I’ve felt really distressed and wanted the food out of me, or wanted to feel less full, less… wrong in my body, I guess. But whether that means I’ve actually done those things for weight control — I don’t know. My mind sort of separates the thoughts from the actions, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd with laxatives, I get hung up on what counts, because if someone takes something because they feel physically uncomfortable, is that the same as doing it for weight? I don’t want to answer in a way that makes it sound more deliberate than it felt at the time.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure. I can say I’ve had the fear and the urge to undo eating, but I can’t clearly say yes, I’ve made myself vomit or taken laxatives specifically to control my weight or shape.\n\nDoctor: When you feel distressed after eating or feel too full, do you ever restrict your food intake significantly afterwards or push yourself to exercise more than usual to balance it out?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, I do restrict afterward. Or maybe it’s not even just “afterward,” because most days I’m already trying to keep it very controlled from the start.\n\nLike on workdays, especially with the early shift, I’ll usually just have coffee before I go in. I tell myself it’s because it’s early and I don’t have time, or because eating before working makes me feel heavy, but honestly it’s also because if I eat first thing then the whole day feels like it’s already gone off track. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar — not always even the whole thing — because I can kind of justify that as practical, like fuel enough to keep going but not enough to feel like I’ve messed up.\n\nAnd then later, if I go to yoga, I feel like I can have something small after, usually just a bowl of vegetables. That feels safer. It feels contained. If I eat more than that, or something heavier, I get this panicky feeling like the day is out of control and I need to fix it somehow. So I guess when I feel too full or distressed, yes, I try to make up for it by eating a lot less afterward, and exercise can become part of that balance too. But the food part is definitely the main thing — keeping it as little as I can so I don’t feel like I’ve let everything get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately how much you weigh now, and how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t actually like saying the number out loud. Occupational health has it written down, because they weighed me there, and that’s partly why I got sent here. They said it was dangerously low for someone doing my job — I run presses and do fabrication, so I’m on my feet and moving around all day — and they seemed much more alarmed than I expected.\n\nI honestly thought I was just getting leaner, like more disciplined, more efficient in my body, if that makes sense. Not “dangerously” anything. So when they reacted that way, it felt embarrassing and kind of… like I’d misread the situation completely.\n\nAs for height, I’m not very tall, but I know you need the actual numbers. I just feel uncomfortable saying them because then it becomes this official thing. But the main reason I’m here is that they think my weight is too low for my height and the kind of physical work I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you share with me your current weight in pounds or kilograms and your height in feet/inches or meters so I can better understand your health metrics?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for the exact numbers, and I understand why, I just… I’m having a really hard time saying them out loud. It feels like once I say the number, that becomes the whole focus, and I start feeling really exposed.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health weighed me, and that’s why they sent me here. They told me my weight was dangerously low for someone doing my job — running presses, doing fabrication, being on my feet and moving around all day. They seemed genuinely worried, more worried than I thought they would be.\n\nI honestly didn’t see it that way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control of myself. I didn’t think of it as dangerous. So hearing them say that was really unsettling.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not the clean answer you’re asking for. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just don’t feel able to give the exact weight and height right this second.\n\nDoctor: Would you be willing to share your current weight and height with me so I can calculate your BMI and better understand your overall health status?\n\nPatient: I know you need the actual number to calculate it, and I’m sorry, I’m still… I’m still having trouble saying it out loud. It’s not that I don’t understand why you’re asking. I do. I know BMI is a medical thing and you’re trying to understand the risk, not judge me.\n\nWhat I can tell you is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for what I do all day. I run presses and do fabrication work, so I’m not just sitting at a desk — I’m on my feet, moving, lifting, concentrating around equipment. They made it sound like my body wasn’t in a safe place for that kind of work.\n\nBut honestly, before they said it that way, I didn’t think of it as “dangerously low.” I really thought I was just getting leaner. More disciplined. Like I had more control over myself. So hearing them frame it as a health concern kind of threw me, and now saying the number feels like admitting something I’m not fully ready to look at directly.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your current weight is significantly lower than what would be considered healthy for someone of your height, based on what occupational health told you?\n\nPatient: Yes… based on what occupational health told me, yes, they seem to think it’s significantly lower than it should be for my height. They used words like “dangerously low,” which honestly still feels hard to hear, because in my head I wasn’t thinking of it that way at all.\n\nI thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled, more disciplined — especially with the kind of work I do, being on my feet all day, running presses, doing fabrication, keeping busy. I guess I saw it as being efficient or keeping myself in line, not as something unsafe.\n\nBut they were very clear that, for someone doing that kind of physical work, they were concerned about my weight. So… yes, I suppose the answer is yes, even if part of me still has trouble accepting it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as missing periods or having them stop altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I know what you’re asking, whether they’ve become irregular or stopped, but I haven’t been tracking it closely in a way where I can give you a confident timeline or anything.\n\nI guess I’ve been so focused on work and on keeping everything controlled day to day that I haven’t paid as much attention to that as maybe I should have. If something was a little off, I don’t know that I would have written it down or connected it to anything. So… I don’t want to say something definite when I’m not sure.\n\nI can think about it more carefully if that helps, but right now I don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you missed any periods or noticed that they have stopped for several months?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know for certain. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I’m not trying to. I just haven’t been keeping track of it in a careful way, like marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\nThere may have been gaps where I thought, “Oh, it’s been a while,” but then I’d get busy with work or just push the thought aside because it felt like one more thing I didn’t want to look at too closely. So I can’t honestly say, yes, it stopped for several months, because I don’t know that with confidence.\n\nI guess the most accurate answer is that I haven’t noticed it in a clear, definite way, and I haven’t tracked it well enough to give you a reliable timeline.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period, even if you didn’t realize it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really can’t say for sure. I know you’re asking it a bit differently — like, even if I didn’t clock it at the time, could there have been a stretch of three months or more — and honestly, that’s the part I don’t feel confident answering.\n\nI haven’t kept track properly. I didn’t write dates down, and I wasn’t really paying attention in a way where I could look back and say, “Yes, that definitely happened.” There might have been a longer gap, I suppose, but I don’t want to say yes to three months if I’m just guessing.\n\nIt’s one of those things where I think I pushed it out of my mind because I was focused on getting through work and keeping my routine. So… I’m not saying it definitely didn’t happen, I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period at any point in the past year?\n\nPatient: I still don’t think I can answer that with certainty, even putting it within the past year. I know that sounds frustrating because you’re asking a pretty specific thing, but I just… I haven’t kept track in a way that would let me say, “Yes, there was a three-month stretch,” or “No, there definitely wasn’t.”\n\nI can look back and think there were times where I wasn’t really aware of it happening, or I’d vaguely think maybe it had been a while, but I didn’t mark it down, and I didn’t really want to focus on it. It was sort of one of those things I pushed to the side because I had other things I was trying to manage and I didn’t want another thing to worry about.\n\nSo the honest answer is: I don’t know. It may have happened, but I can’t confirm three months or more in the past year.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year, was there ever a time when you went three months or more without having your period, even if you didn’t realize it at the time?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve kind of answered around this already, but… looking back over the past year, I really still can’t say for sure that there was a full three months where I didn’t have one.\n\nIt’s not that I remember clearly and the answer is no — it’s more that I wasn’t tracking it properly. I wasn’t writing anything down, and I think there were stretches where I just wasn’t paying attention to it, or I’d notice vaguely that it seemed like it had been a while and then not follow that thought because it made me uncomfortable or I was busy.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether it could have happened without me realizing it, I guess… maybe, yes, it could have. But I don’t want to say that it definitely did, because I honestly don’t have a clear memory or a record of it. The most honest answer I can give is that I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, can you confirm whether there has been a time in the past year when you went three months or more without a menstrual period?\n\nPatient: I can’t confirm it, no. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not the clean answer you’re trying to get, but looking back over the past year, I don’t have enough of a record in my head to say, “Yes, that definitely happened.”\n\nI wasn’t tracking my periods properly — not on a calendar, not on an app, nothing like that — and I think there were times where I just sort of… didn’t want to notice too closely. Like if it seemed late or absent, I might have had a vague thought about it, but then I’d push it away rather than count the weeks.\n\nSo I can say it’s possible there was a longer gap, but I can’t honestly confirm three months or more. I just don’t know for certain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m still not fairly certain, no. I know you’re trying to get at whether there was at least one clear three-month stretch, even if I wasn’t tracking it at the time, and I wish I could look back and say, “Yes, that block of time was definitely without a period,” but I really can’t.

So… no, there isn’t a specific three-month period I can point to where I’m fairly certain I didn’t have one. It might have happened, but I can’t say that with confidence.

I just don’t know for certain.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year, is there any three-month period where you're fairly certain you did not have a menstrual period, even if you weren't actively keeping track of it?\n\nPatient: I’m still not fairly certain, no. I know you’re trying to get at whether there was at least one clear three-month stretch, even if I wasn’t tracking it at the time, and I wish I could look back and say, “Yes, that block of time was definitely without a period,” but I really can’t.\n\nThe problem is that my memory around it is kind of patchy. Not like I blacked out or anything, just that I didn’t treat it as something I was supposed to keep records of. I’d notice things in a vague way, like, “Huh, maybe it’s been a while,” but then I wouldn’t count backward or mark anything down. And honestly, if I started thinking about it too much, I’d get uncomfortable and just move on to something else.\n\nSo… no, there isn’t a specific three-month period I can point to where I’m fairly certain I didn’t have one. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Absolutely sure? No… no, I can’t say that. I know that sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m really not trying to. I just don’t have a clean memory of it that way.

If you asked me whether I tracked it carefully, the answer is definitely no. If you asked whether there were times when I wondered if it had been a while, then yes, I think there were. But “absolutely sure there was a full three-month period with no period at all” — I can’t honestly say that.

Part of the problem is that I wasn’t marking it down, and I think I avoided thinking about it too closely. So now, looking back, everything sort of blurs together. I can’t point to a specific season or set of months and say, “Yes, that whole time, nothing happened.” It may have, but I don’t know for sure.

I don’t always want to admit that because it sounds shallow, and I tell myself it’s more about discipline or routine or feeling in control, but the second the scale goes up even a little, I panic.\n\nLike, even a small change — something anyone else would probably say is nothing — my brain immediately goes, “This is it, you’re losing control, it’s all going to come back, you’re going to get heavy again.” And then I can’t stop thinking about it. It feels like all the work I’ve done to keep myself contained could just disappear if I let up at all.\n\nSo yes, body shape too, I guess, but the scale is the thing that makes it feel real. If the number goes up, I feel like I’ve failed and I have to tighten everything back down again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself sick or use laxatives after eating, or push yourself to exercise more than you should to control your weight or shape?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure how to answer that neatly.\n\nI don’t really think of it in those exact categories, like “making myself sick” or “using laxatives” or whatever. That’s not something I’ve been keeping track of in the way I track food or the scale. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but it feels hard because some things I do feel normal to me, and then when someone says it back in a medical-sounding way it suddenly sounds more serious.\n\nWith exercise… I do get anxious if I don’t move enough, and I can get pretty rigid about it. Like if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I’ll feel this pressure in my head that I need to compensate somehow. But I don’t know where the line is between “I like being active” and “more than I should.” I work a physical job already, so it’s all kind of mixed together in my mind.\n\nI guess what I can say is that I do pay a lot of attention to making sure I don’t feel like food is just sitting there with no way to balance it out. But I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives to prevent weight gain or to feel like you’ve ‘cleansed’ after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that in a way that’s useful.\n\nI’m not saying yes, exactly. I don’t have some clear thing where I can say, “Oh, I do this many times a week,” or “I use this to undo eating.” It’s not like that in my head. And I don’t want to say something wrong and have it sound like I’m admitting to something if I’m not even sure what counts.\n\nI understand what you mean by making yourself vomit, but… I guess I don’t think of myself as someone who does that. And with laxatives, I’m not sure what you mean by “used” — like taking them because you’re constipated, or taking them because you feel awful after eating, or taking more than you’re supposed to? Because those feel like different things to me, and I don’t really have a neat answer.\n\nThe “cleansed” part is hard too, because I do understand that feeling of wanting food to be gone, or wanting to feel empty again, but I don’t know that I can connect it to those specific things you’re asking about. I think I mostly just get stuck in my head afterward, like trying to figure out how to make the feeling pass. So… I’m not sure. I don’t want to say yes if that’s not accurate.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or taken laxatives specifically to try to control your weight or shape, even if it was just once or twice?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know you’re trying to make it more specific, but I still don’t feel like I can give you a clean yes-or-no.\n\nI don’t have a clear memory where I think, “Yes, I did that specifically to change my weight or shape.” At least not in a way I can confidently say out loud. I know I’ve had times after eating where I’ve felt really distressed and wanted the food out of me, or wanted to feel less full, less… wrong in my body, I guess. But whether that means I’ve actually done those things for weight control — I don’t know. My mind sort of separates the thoughts from the actions, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd with laxatives, I get hung up on what counts, because if someone takes something because they feel physically uncomfortable, is that the same as doing it for weight? I don’t want to answer in a way that makes it sound more deliberate than it felt at the time.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure. I can say I’ve had the fear and the urge to undo eating, but I can’t clearly say yes, I’ve made myself vomit or taken laxatives specifically to control my weight or shape.\n\nDoctor: When you feel distressed after eating or feel too full, do you ever restrict your food intake significantly afterwards or push yourself to exercise more than usual to balance it out?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, I do restrict afterward. Or maybe it’s not even just “afterward,” because most days I’m already trying to keep it very controlled from the start.\n\nLike on workdays, especially with the early shift, I’ll usually just have coffee before I go in. I tell myself it’s because it’s early and I don’t have time, or because eating before working makes me feel heavy, but honestly it’s also because if I eat first thing then the whole day feels like it’s already gone off track. Then at lunch I might have, like, half a protein bar — not always even the whole thing — because I can kind of justify that as practical, like fuel enough to keep going but not enough to feel like I’ve messed up.\n\nAnd then later, if I go to yoga, I feel like I can have something small after, usually just a bowl of vegetables. That feels safer. It feels contained. If I eat more than that, or something heavier, I get this panicky feeling like the day is out of control and I need to fix it somehow. So I guess when I feel too full or distressed, yes, I try to make up for it by eating a lot less afterward, and exercise can become part of that balance too. But the food part is definitely the main thing — keeping it as little as I can so I don’t feel like I’ve let everything get away from me.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me approximately how much you weigh now, and how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t actually like saying the number out loud. Occupational health has it written down, because they weighed me there, and that’s partly why I got sent here. They said it was dangerously low for someone doing my job — I run presses and do fabrication, so I’m on my feet and moving around all day — and they seemed much more alarmed than I expected.\n\nI honestly thought I was just getting leaner, like more disciplined, more efficient in my body, if that makes sense. Not “dangerously” anything. So when they reacted that way, it felt embarrassing and kind of… like I’d misread the situation completely.\n\nAs for height, I’m not very tall, but I know you need the actual numbers. I just feel uncomfortable saying them because then it becomes this official thing. But the main reason I’m here is that they think my weight is too low for my height and the kind of physical work I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Can you share with me your current weight in pounds or kilograms and your height in feet/inches or meters so I can better understand your health metrics?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for the exact numbers, and I understand why, I just… I’m having a really hard time saying them out loud. It feels like once I say the number, that becomes the whole focus, and I start feeling really exposed.\n\nWhat I can say is that occupational health weighed me, and that’s why they sent me here. They told me my weight was dangerously low for someone doing my job — running presses, doing fabrication, being on my feet and moving around all day. They seemed genuinely worried, more worried than I thought they would be.\n\nI honestly didn’t see it that way. I thought I was just getting leaner, more disciplined, like I had better control of myself. I didn’t think of it as dangerous. So hearing them say that was really unsettling.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not the clean answer you’re asking for. I’m not trying to be difficult. I just don’t feel able to give the exact weight and height right this second.\n\nDoctor: Would you be willing to share your current weight and height with me so I can calculate your BMI and better understand your overall health status?\n\nPatient: I know you need the actual number to calculate it, and I’m sorry, I’m still… I’m still having trouble saying it out loud. It’s not that I don’t understand why you’re asking. I do. I know BMI is a medical thing and you’re trying to understand the risk, not judge me.\n\nWhat I can tell you is that occupational health sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low for what I do all day. I run presses and do fabrication work, so I’m not just sitting at a desk — I’m on my feet, moving, lifting, concentrating around equipment. They made it sound like my body wasn’t in a safe place for that kind of work.\n\nBut honestly, before they said it that way, I didn’t think of it as “dangerously low.” I really thought I was just getting leaner. More disciplined. Like I had more control over myself. So hearing them frame it as a health concern kind of threw me, and now saying the number feels like admitting something I’m not fully ready to look at directly.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your current weight is significantly lower than what would be considered healthy for someone of your height, based on what occupational health told you?\n\nPatient: Yes… based on what occupational health told me, yes, they seem to think it’s significantly lower than it should be for my height. They used words like “dangerously low,” which honestly still feels hard to hear, because in my head I wasn’t thinking of it that way at all.\n\nI thought I was just getting leaner, more controlled, more disciplined — especially with the kind of work I do, being on my feet all day, running presses, doing fabrication, keeping busy. I guess I saw it as being efficient or keeping myself in line, not as something unsafe.\n\nBut they were very clear that, for someone doing that kind of physical work, they were concerned about my weight. So… yes, I suppose the answer is yes, even if part of me still has trouble accepting it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as missing periods or having them stop altogether?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I know what you’re asking, whether they’ve become irregular or stopped, but I haven’t been tracking it closely in a way where I can give you a confident timeline or anything.\n\nI guess I’ve been so focused on work and on keeping everything controlled day to day that I haven’t paid as much attention to that as maybe I should have. If something was a little off, I don’t know that I would have written it down or connected it to anything. So… I don’t want to say something definite when I’m not sure.\n\nI can think about it more carefully if that helps, but right now I don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you missed any periods or noticed that they have stopped for several months?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know for certain. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I’m not trying to. I just haven’t been keeping track of it in a careful way, like marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\nThere may have been gaps where I thought, “Oh, it’s been a while,” but then I’d get busy with work or just push the thought aside because it felt like one more thing I didn’t want to look at too closely. So I can’t honestly say, yes, it stopped for several months, because I don’t know that with confidence.\n\nI guess the most accurate answer is that I haven’t noticed it in a clear, definite way, and I haven’t tracked it well enough to give you a reliable timeline.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period, even if you didn’t realize it at the time?\n\nPatient: I really can’t say for sure. I know you’re asking it a bit differently — like, even if I didn’t clock it at the time, could there have been a stretch of three months or more — and honestly, that’s the part I don’t feel confident answering.\n\nI haven’t kept track properly. I didn’t write dates down, and I wasn’t really paying attention in a way where I could look back and say, “Yes, that definitely happened.” There might have been a longer gap, I suppose, but I don’t want to say yes to three months if I’m just guessing.\n\nIt’s one of those things where I think I pushed it out of my mind because I was focused on getting through work and keeping my routine. So… I’m not saying it definitely didn’t happen, I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you gone three months or more without having a period at any point in the past year?\n\nPatient: I still don’t think I can answer that with certainty, even putting it within the past year. I know that sounds frustrating because you’re asking a pretty specific thing, but I just… I haven’t kept track in a way that would let me say, “Yes, there was a three-month stretch,” or “No, there definitely wasn’t.”\n\nI can look back and think there were times where I wasn’t really aware of it happening, or I’d vaguely think maybe it had been a while, but I didn’t mark it down, and I didn’t really want to focus on it. It was sort of one of those things I pushed to the side because I had other things I was trying to manage and I didn’t want another thing to worry about.\n\nSo the honest answer is: I don’t know. It may have happened, but I can’t confirm three months or more in the past year.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year, was there ever a time when you went three months or more without having your period, even if you didn’t realize it at the time?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve kind of answered around this already, but… looking back over the past year, I really still can’t say for sure that there was a full three months where I didn’t have one.\n\nIt’s not that I remember clearly and the answer is no — it’s more that I wasn’t tracking it properly. I wasn’t writing anything down, and I think there were stretches where I just wasn’t paying attention to it, or I’d notice vaguely that it seemed like it had been a while and then not follow that thought because it made me uncomfortable or I was busy.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether it could have happened without me realizing it, I guess… maybe, yes, it could have. But I don’t want to say that it definitely did, because I honestly don’t have a clear memory or a record of it. The most honest answer I can give is that I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, can you confirm whether there has been a time in the past year when you went three months or more without a menstrual period?\n\nPatient: I can’t confirm it, no. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not the clean answer you’re trying to get, but looking back over the past year, I don’t have enough of a record in my head to say, “Yes, that definitely happened.”\n\nI wasn’t tracking my periods properly — not on a calendar, not on an app, nothing like that — and I think there were times where I just sort of… didn’t want to notice too closely. Like if it seemed late or absent, I might have had a vague thought about it, but then I’d push it away rather than count the weeks.\n\nSo I can say it’s possible there was a longer gap, but I can’t honestly confirm three months or more. I just don’t know for certain.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year, is there any three-month period where you're fairly certain you did not have a menstrual period, even if you weren't actively keeping track of it?\n\nPatient: I’m still not fairly certain, no. I know you’re trying to get at whether there was at least one clear three-month stretch, even if I wasn’t tracking it at the time, and I wish I could look back and say, “Yes, that block of time was definitely without a period,” but I really can’t.\n\nThe problem is that my memory around it is kind of patchy. Not like I blacked out or anything, just that I didn’t treat it as something I was supposed to keep records of. I’d notice things in a vague way, like, “Huh, maybe it’s been a while,” but then I wouldn’t count backward or mark anything down. And honestly, if I started thinking about it too much, I’d get uncomfortable and just move on to something else.\n\nSo… no, there isn’t a specific three-month period I can point to where I’m fairly certain I didn’t have one. It might have happened, but I can’t say that with confidence.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past year, is there any three-month period where you're absolutely sure you did not have a menstrual period, even if you weren't keeping track of it at the time?\n\nPatient: Absolutely sure? No… no, I can’t say that. I know that sounds like I’m dodging it, but I’m really not trying to. I just don’t have a clean memory of it that way.\n\nIf you asked me whether I tracked it carefully, the answer is definitely no. If you asked whether there were times when I wondered if it had been a while, then yes, I think there were. But “absolutely sure there was a full three-month period with no period at all” — I can’t honestly say that.\n\nPart of the problem is that I wasn’t marking it down, and I think I avoided thinking about it too closely. So now, looking back, everything sort of blurs together. I can’t point to a specific season or set of months and say, “Yes, that whole time, nothing happened.” It may have, but I don’t know for sure."
